Just my name, clear as day. \n\nBut it's never cruel or mean. It doesn't tell me I'm a bad person or that I'm failing at the business or anything awful like that. Honestly, the guilt I feel myself about not being productive enough right now, about just staring at my trays and not making anything... well, that's already heavy enough on its own. I certainly do a fine enough job making myself feel guilty, I don't need a voice doing it for me! \n\nBut the voice itself, no. It’s just a woman, sounding almost exactly like a customer who just wandered in from the street and is trying to get my attention from the front display cases while I'm working in the back. Like she's just stepping out of the rain and calling out to see if anyone is minding the store. It’s never full sentences or anything critical or degrading like you’re asking about. It's just... just my name, hanging there in the air, and then when I get up and walk out to the showroom to see who it is, wiping the polishing compound off my hands, there's absolutely no one there. Sometimes I wish someone else would take over, honestly, especially when I'm sitting at my bench trying to figure out a new setting for a cabochon and I just can't focus. I'll be staring at a piece of silver bezel wire, knowing exactly how I need to measure it and cut it and solder it, but my brain just feels like it's wading through molasses. And then I start thinking about the suppliers and the bills and whether those people looking in the window are checking up on me... but those are definitely my thoughts. My worries. \n\nNo one is forcing them on me or planting them there like seeds. It's just my own mind, running away with itself, or... or just grinding to a complete halt, depending on the day. Just... very tired, and very overwhelmed by my own mind, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed mood lasted for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, my... it's been much, much longer than that. It feels like it's been stretching on forever, honestly. It just stays with me while I'm brushing my teeth, while I'm trying to force down a piece of toast, and it follows me all the way to the store. \n\nI unlock the front door, turn off the alarm, flip on the overhead lights over the display cases, and I just stand there feeling completely hollowed out. I'll drop whatever I'm doing and run over to the soldering station to make sure I didn't leave a torch on or that a valve isn't leaking. I'll check the dials, I'll check the hoses, I'll even touch the tips of the torches to see if they're warm. But they're always stone cold. Everything is off. But the smell is just so real, and the voice is so real, that I just have to go check every single time, even though part of me knows by now that I'm probably going to find an empty room. It's just so unsettling, you know? To experience something so vividly and then find out it's just... not there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? Oh, no. No, I've never had a seizure. I mean, I know what they look like, of course. My neighbor, well, my old neighbor from about fifteen years ago, her name was Brenda, she had this little terrier mix that used to have seizures. Poor thing would just fall over in the yard and shake, and Brenda would get so upset, naturally. She'd have to sit there on the grass with him until it passed. But me? No, nothing like that. \n\nI've always been pretty healthy in that regard. I mean, working with my hands all day, doing such fine, detailed work with the stones and the metal, you really need a steady hand. If I were having seizures, or even just tremors, I don't think I could do my job at all. Setting a tiny diamond into a prong setting takes so much precision, you know? You have to hold the piece just right, apply just the right amount of pressure with the tools. So, no, definitely no seizures. My hands are steady... it's just my thoughts and my words that seem to be getting all tangled up lately. But physically, no, no seizures or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually irritable lately?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Well, I wouldn't say I've been unusually irritable, no. I mean, sure, everyone gets a little annoyed now and then, especially when things don't go according to plan. Like last Tuesday, I had this customer come in—a very nice lady, don't get me wrong, she's been coming to me for years—but she brought in this antique filigree ring that her grandmother had given her, and she wanted me to resize it. Now, resizing filigree is always tricky because the metal is so delicate, and if you aren't careful, you can completely ruin the pattern. So I explained all of this to her, very patiently, and told her it would take at least two weeks because I needed to order a specific type of gold solder to match the alloy perfectly. \n\nBut then, not even three days later, she calls the shop wanting to know if it's ready! I had to explain the whole process to her all over again. And yes, in that moment, I suppose I felt a tiny bit of frustration, just because I had been so clear the first time. But I wasn't snapping at her or losing my temper. I kept my voice perfectly level and polite, just like I always do. \n\nAnd my sister, she's always telling me I have the patience of a saint, especially with the intricate work I do. You really can't be an irritable person and sit at a bench for six hours straight, peering through a loupe, trying to set a one-millimeter sapphire without scratching the surface. It requires a very calm, very centered disposition. So, no, I don't think I've been feeling unusually irritable. Just the normal, everyday little annoyances, but nothing out of the ordinary, and certainly nothing I can't handle with a deep breath and a cup of tea. It's really just the sadness and the... the confusion with my words and those strange moments in the quiet shop that are bothering me. But my temper? No, that's been perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, I have. It’s funny you phrase it like that, \"most of the day, nearly every day,\" because that’s exactly what it feels like. It’s this constant, lingering thing. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has just completely drained out of the house before I even get out of bed, let alone before I get to the shop. \n\nIt’s hard to explain, really. You know how when you look at a beautiful piece of jewelry, maybe a really nice opal, and it has all that fire and play of color? That’s how life used to feel to me. There were bright spots, things to look forward to, even just small things like a good cup of coffee or a nice chat with a customer. But now, it’s like looking at a piece of rough, unpolished quartz. Just dull and gray. \n\nI’ll lie there in bed and look at the ceiling, and the thought of getting up and going through the motions just feels so... monumental. I still do it, of course, because I have the shop to run and my sister relies on me to keep things going, but the joy is just gone. I’ll sit at my bench, and even when I’m working on something I used to love, like a really intricate custom setting, I just feel this weight sitting right on my chest. It doesn't go away after lunch, it doesn't go away when I close up the shop for the evening. It’s just this quiet, heavy sadness that follows me around, day in and day out. So, yes, I would say I’ve been feeling depressed almost constantly lately. It’s exhausting, to be honest. Just exhausting to feel so empty all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the burning-metal smell ever happened during a time when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Well... yes, actually. Yes, they have. It’s strange to think back on it now, because the sadness feels like it’s been here forever, but there was a time before this heavy feeling really settled in. I was actually doing quite well, keeping very busy. I was still running my evening jewelry-making classes at the community center—which I loved doing, teaching people how to solder and work with silver clay, it’s so rewarding to see their faces when they finish their first piece. And I was gardening, too. I have these beautiful heirloom tomatoes I was tending to, and some lovely climbing roses along the trellis in the back. \n\nBut even then, when I was feeling perfectly fine, not depressed at all, I was having these... experiences. It went on for several weeks, I think. I would be in the shop, feeling completely normal, maybe just organizing my tools or sketching a new design, and I kept hearing these noises coming from the polishing room. It sounded like the tumbling machine was running, or like someone was moving the buffs around, but I was the only one there. And the smell of the burning metal would just waft in, so strong I’d have to check the torches even though I hadn't used them all day. \n\nAnd during that same time, I started getting so worried that customers were... watching the business. Not just looking in the windows, but really watching. I’d see someone pause outside the shop, maybe admiring a necklace in the display, but I’d get this overwhelming feeling that they were monitoring me, keeping track of my inventory or my hours. I even started keeping the blinds half-drawn during the day, which is terrible for a retail shop, I know, but I just couldn't shake the feeling. So, yes, all of that—the noises, the smells, the worrying—that was happening for weeks while I was still feeling quite energetic and involved with my classes and my garden, long before this terrible sadness took over.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a fairly consistent sleeper, really. I mean, my sister always jokes that I run like clockwork, and it's true. Usually, I'm in bed by 10:30, maybe 11:00 if I've gotten caught up reading a good book—lately I've been reading this fascinating biography of René Lalique, his glasswork is just incredible, the way he incorporated those natural motifs into the Art Nouveau style, it really makes you think about how we approach design today. But anyway, yes, I'm usually asleep by 11:00 and up by 7:00, because the shop opens at 9:30 and I like to have a good hour and a half to myself in the morning to have my tea, read the paper, and just ease into the day before I have to start dealing with customers and vendors and all of that. \n\nEven back when I was feeling good, when I was teaching those evening classes and spending all my weekends out in the garden with the tomatoes and the roses, I still needed my full eight hours. I might have been busy and active, but I was definitely tired at the end of the day. In fact, teaching those classes takes a lot out of you, emotionally and physically, hovering over people's shoulders, making sure they don't burn themselves with the micro-torches... by the time I'd get home at 9:00 PM, I'd be ready to just collapse. So, no, I've never had a time where I was just buzzing along on three or four hours of sleep and feeling perfectly fine. If I don't get my rest, my hands get shaky, and you absolutely cannot have shaky hands when you're trying to set a tiny little diamond into a delicate prong setting. It's just not possible. So, no, I've always needed my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when the voices or suspicious thoughts lasted for at least two weeks while you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually, just like I was saying earlier... it's funny how the mind works, isn't it? You kind of lose track of the timeline when you're in the thick of this heavy sadness, but yes, there was definitely a stretch of time before the depression really settled in. I was feeling quite myself, honestly. I was still running my evening classes at the community center, teaching the silver clay and the soldering, which I always found so fulfilling. And I was out in the garden every weekend, tying up those heirloom tomatoes—they get so heavy if you don't support them properly—and tending to the climbing roses. I felt perfectly fine, mood-wise. \n\nBut even then, while I was feeling good, that's when those things started happening, and it went on for several weeks, easily. I would be in the shop, feeling completely normal, maybe sorting through a new batch of semi-precious stones that had just come in from my supplier in Jaipur—they do the most wonderful cuts on their amethysts, really top-notch work—and I'd just keep hearing these noises coming from the polishing room in the back. It was so distinct, like the tumbling machine was running or someone was shuffling the buffs around, but I was entirely alone in the store. \n\nAnd that was the exact same time I started getting so incredibly worried that people were watching the business. It wasn't just a fleeting thought, it was this persistent, gnawing worry for weeks. I'd see a customer outside, maybe just looking at the window display, and I'd be absolutely convinced they were monitoring my inventory or keeping track of when I opened and closed. I was so convinced of it that I started keeping the front blinds half-drawn during regular business hours, which my sister told me was ridiculous for a retail shop, but I just couldn't help it. So, yes, all of that—the noises from the back room, the intense worrying about being watched—that all went on for several weeks while I was still gardening and teaching, well before this awful, draining sadness took over my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Talking less? Well... I suppose it might seem like that to some people, but I don't really think so. I mean, my sister called the other day, and we were on the phone for a good forty-five minutes talking about our aunt's estate sale. It's such a complicated situation, trying to divide up all those antique furniture pieces without causing a family rift. Aunt Helen had this gorgeous Victorian settee, beautifully carved mahogany with the original horsehair stuffing, and my cousin Sarah thinks she should have it just because she has a larger living room, which is completely beside the point if you ask me. \n\nSo, no, when I have something to say, or when I'm talking to my sister or my suppliers, I still talk just as much as I ever did. I was just on the phone with my metal supplier yesterday, actually, trying to sort out a mix-up with an order of sterling silver wire. They sent me half-round instead of dead-soft round, and you simply cannot use half-round when you're trying to do delicate wire-wrapping for pendants, it just doesn't sit right against the stones. I had to explain the whole process to the customer service representative, who clearly had no background in jewelry making whatsoever, bless his heart. I must have spent twenty minutes just trying to get him to understand the difference in the gauges and the temper of the metal. \n\nNow, I will say, because of this heavy, awful sadness I've been feeling, I haven't been going out to social events or chatting up the neighbors over the fence like I used to. When you feel this drained, this utterly exhausted down to your bones, the idea of making small talk about the weather or the local school board elections just feels impossible. It takes so much energy to put on a bright face and pretend everything is fine when inside you just feel like a lead weight. But as far as my actual ability to talk, or how much I speak when I do have a conversation... no, I haven't noticed any change there. I still have plenty to say, it's just that I don't always have the energy to find an audience for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... oh, heavens, yes. That's actually been one of the hardest parts of all this, to be completely honest with you. It really is. \n\nI used to absolutely love—I mean, it was practically my favorite weekend activity—hunting for old clasps. You know, going to estate sales or those big antique fairs out in the county, sifting through boxes of broken vintage jewelry just to find a beautiful, intact Art Deco box clasp with the original paste stones, or maybe a really delicate Victorian barrel clasp. There's just something so thrilling about finding a piece of history like that, rescuing it from a dusty cardboard box, and thinking about how you can incorporate it into a modern design. I could spend hours doing that. And the classes, too. I used to get so much joy out of planning those little jewelry classes I mentioned earlier. I'd sit at my kitchen table with a pot of Earl Grey and draw up all the diagrams, figure out the exact amount of silver clay each student would need, make little sample pendants to show them what we were aiming for... I just loved doing that. \n\nBut lately... I don't know, it's like a switch has been flipped inside me. I'll sit down at my workbench—I have this beautiful vintage oak jeweler's bench with the canvas catch tray underneath—and I'll pull out my organizers, but... I mostly just stare at the trays. I look at all those beautiful clasps I spent so much time hunting down, and the beads, and the wire, and I just feel like none of it is worth starting. It's this overwhelming, heavy feeling of 'why bother?' I'll sit there under the magnifier lamp for maybe twenty minutes, just looking at the little compartments, maybe I'll move a piece of turquoise from one slot to another, and then I just push the tray away. It just feels so utterly pointless right now. It breaks my heart, really, because my work has always been my passion, but the drive to actually create something, to put the pieces together... it's just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you felt depressed for at least two weeks but did not have the voices, unusual smells, or fears of being watched?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not that I can recall... it really didn’t happen like that. Actually, it was quite the opposite, if I'm being honest. When I think back to those worst months, you know, when that heavy, heavy sadness was just pressing down on me so hard that I couldn't even bring myself to care about my bench or my tools... that was exactly when all those other strange things were happening, too. They were all happening at the exact same time. \n\nI’d be sitting there in the shop, just completely drained, feeling that total loss of interest in everything I usually love, and out of nowhere, I would hear my name being called. Just clear as a bell, echoing right off the glass display cases. And there wouldn't be a soul around. It's such an unsettling feeling, being so low and then having your mind play tricks on you like that.\n\nAnd the worry... oh, the worry was just eating me alive right alongside the sadness. I was absolutely, one hundred percent sure that the customers coming in weren't there to look at the jewelry at all. I’d see someone lingering by the front window, maybe looking at a display of turquoise pendants I’d put out, and I just knew—I was completely convinced—that they were checking up on our payments. It’s a tough business, retail, especially with precious metals because the overhead is so astronomical. You have to buy all your silver casting grain and your stones up front from the suppliers, so money is always on your mind anyway. \n\nBut this was different. I’d be sitting there, too depressed to even pick up a pair of pliers, hearing my name in an empty room, and absolutely terrified that every person who walked through the door was secretly auditing my finances or making sure I was paying the bills. It was exhausting. So, no, the depression didn't happen by itself without those other things. It was all just... tangled up together into one big, awful mess during those worst months. I couldn't separate the sadness from the voices or the fear if I tried.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the woman's voice call your name, does it ever say anything about you being worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: Worthless or guilty? Oh, no... no, nothing like that at all. It’s actually quite brief, really. It’s literally just my name. \n\nI’ll be sitting there at the bench—usually, it happens when the shop is very quiet, you know, like on a Tuesday afternoon when foot traffic is just completely dead and I'm trying to get caught up on my finishing work. I use this Foredom flex shaft tool with these little silicone polishing wheels, and even though the motor has a bit of a hum to it, I'll just hear the voice cut right through it. Just my name, clear as day. \n\nBut it's never cruel or mean. It doesn't tell me I'm a bad person or that I'm failing at the business or anything awful like that. Honestly, the guilt I feel myself about not being productive enough right now, about just staring at my trays and not making anything... well, that's already heavy enough on its own. I certainly do a fine enough job making myself feel guilty, I don't need a voice doing it for me! \n\nBut the voice itself, no. It’s just a woman, sounding almost exactly like a customer who just wandered in from the street and is trying to get my attention from the front display cases while I'm working in the back. Like she's just stepping out of the rain and calling out to see if anyone is minding the store. It’s never full sentences or anything critical or degrading like you’re asking about. It's just... just my name, hanging there in the air, and then when I get up and walk out to the showroom to see who it is, wiping the polishing compound off my hands, there's absolutely no one there. But no, it never says I'm worthless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts or putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? Or putting them in my head? Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that. I mean, I know my thoughts are my own, even when they're... well, even when they're a bit jumbled or when that heavy sadness takes over. \n\nI know some people talk about things like that, feeling like they aren't in control of their own minds, but for me, it's really just... it's just me in here. Sometimes I wish someone else would take over, honestly, especially when I'm sitting at my bench trying to figure out a new setting for a cabochon and I just can't focus. I'll be staring at a piece of silver bezel wire, knowing exactly how I need to measure it and cut it and solder it, but my brain just feels like it's wading through molasses. And then I start thinking about the suppliers and the bills and whether those people looking in the window are checking up on me... but those are definitely my thoughts. My worries. \n\nNo one is forcing them on me or planting them there like seeds. It's just my own mind, running away with itself, or... or just grinding to a complete halt, depending on the day. But it's all me. Just me and the quiet in the shop, and occasionally that voice calling my name, but even then, my thoughts are my own. I've never felt like I was a puppet or anything like that. Just... very tired, and very overwhelmed by my own mind, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed mood lasted for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, my... it's been much, much longer than that. It feels like it's been stretching on forever, honestly. It's definitely been going on for weeks and weeks in a row without a break. \n\nEvery single day, it's just the same thing. Most mornings, I wake up and there's just this... this flat, heavy sadness sitting right on my chest. It's like the color has completely drained out of the house before I even get out of bed, let alone before I even make it down to the shop. You know how when you wake up and the sun is shining and you think, 'Oh, I'm going to make a pot of coffee and maybe try that new bezel setting technique I saw in that trade magazine'? Well, it's the exact opposite of that. \n\nI'll just lie there, staring at the ceiling fan—it has these little brass accents on the blades that I used to think were so charming when we first moved in, but now they just look dull—and I just feel so incredibly empty and sad. It's a physical weight, I swear it is. And it doesn't lift. It just stays with me while I'm brushing my teeth, while I'm trying to force down a piece of toast, and it follows me all the way to the store. \n\nI unlock the front door, turn off the alarm, flip on the overhead lights over the display cases, and I just stand there feeling completely hollowed out. It doesn't matter if it's a Monday or a Saturday, the feeling is just constant. So, yes, to answer your question, it has absolutely been at least two weeks. It's just a continuous, unrelenting grayness that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try to push through it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... yes, actually. I think there was. It's funny you ask it that way, because I was just thinking about how things were before this awful, heavy feeling took over everything. 

There was a stretch—I want to say it was earlier this spring, before the depression really settled in and made it impossible to get out of bed. I was actually doing quite well! I was still running my Thursday evening wire-wrapping classes for beginners. I had a full roster, eight ladies, and we were making these lovely little copper and turquoise pendants. And I was gardening on the weekends, too. I had just put in a whole new row of hydrangeas along the side of the house... the big mophead kind, you know? The ones that turn blue if you get the soil acidity just right. I was out there with my trowel and my gardening gloves, feeling perfectly fine, not sad at all. Energetic, even.

But during that same time, for several weeks, I was having those other things happen. I'd be in the middle of teaching a class, demonstrating how to use round-nose pliers to make a perfect loop, and I'd hear those noises coming from the polishing room in the back. Not just the voice calling my name, but like... shuffling, or the sound of the flex shaft motor whirring for a second when I knew I had unplugged it. And that feeling of being watched... oh, that was so strong then. I kept worrying that customers—or not even customers, just people out on the sidewalk—were staring through the front window, scrutinizing the business, judging how I was running things, maybe even plotting to break in and steal the silver stock. 

I was so preoccupied with it that I even went and bought a new deadbolt for the back door near the alley. But my mood? My mood was completely fine. I wasn't crying, I wasn't feeling that terrible gray emptiness. I was just... busy, and gardening, and teaching, but constantly looking over my shoulder and hearing things in the back room. So, yes, there was definitely a period of a few weeks where I had all of that going on, but none of the sadness. It's so strange how the mind works, isn't it? Because now it's all just jumbled together with this awful depression.

I still have plenty to say, it's just that I don't always have the energy to find an audience for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... oh, heavens, yes. That's actually been one of the hardest parts of all this, to be completely honest with you. It really is. \n\nI used to absolutely love—I mean, it was practically my favorite weekend activity—hunting for old clasps. You know, going to estate sales or those big antique fairs out in the county, sifting through boxes of broken vintage jewelry just to find a beautiful, intact Art Deco box clasp with the original paste stones, or maybe a really delicate Victorian barrel clasp. There's just something so thrilling about finding a piece of history like that, rescuing it from a dusty cardboard box, and thinking about how you can incorporate it into a modern design. I could spend hours doing that. And the classes, too. I used to get so much joy out of planning those little jewelry classes I mentioned earlier. I'd sit at my kitchen table with a pot of Earl Grey and draw up all the diagrams, figure out the exact amount of silver clay each student would need, make little sample pendants to show them what we were aiming for... I just loved doing that. \n\nBut lately... I don't know, it's like a switch has been flipped inside me. I'll sit down at my workbench—I have this beautiful vintage oak jeweler's bench with the canvas catch tray underneath—and I'll pull out my organizers, but... I mostly just stare at the trays. I look at all those beautiful clasps I spent so much time hunting down, and the beads, and the wire, and I just feel like none of it is worth starting. It's this overwhelming, heavy feeling of 'why bother?' I'll sit there under the magnifier lamp for maybe twenty minutes, just looking at the little compartments, maybe I'll move a piece of turquoise from one slot to another, and then I just push the tray away. I still have plenty to say, it's just that I don't always have the energy to find an audience for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... oh, heavens, yes. That's actually been one of the hardest parts of all this, to be completely honest with you. It really is. \n\nI used to absolutely love—I mean, it was practically my favorite weekend activity—hunting for old clasps. You know, going to estate sales or those big antique fairs out in the county, sifting through boxes of broken vintage jewelry just to find a beautiful, intact Art Deco box clasp with the original paste stones, or maybe a really delicate Victorian barrel clasp. There's just something so thrilling about finding a piece of history like that, rescuing it from a dusty cardboard box, and thinking about how you can incorporate it into a modern design. I could spend hours doing that. And the classes, too. I used to get so much joy out of planning those little jewelry classes I mentioned earlier. I'd sit at my kitchen table with a pot of Earl Grey and draw up all the diagrams, figure out the exact amount of silver clay each student would need, make little sample pendants to show them what we were aiming for... I just loved doing that. \n\nBut lately... I don't know, it's like a switch has been flipped inside me. I'll sit down at my workbench—I have this beautiful vintage oak jeweler's bench with the canvas catch tray underneath—and I'll pull out my organizers, but... I mostly just stare at the trays. I look at all those beautiful clasps I spent so much time hunting down, and the beads, and the wire, and I just feel like none of it is worth starting. It's this overwhelming, heavy feeling of 'why bother?' I'll sit there under the magnifier lamp for maybe twenty minutes, just looking at the little compartments, maybe I'll move a piece of turquoise from one slot to another, and then I just push the tray away. It just feels so utterly pointless right now. It breaks my heart, really, because my work has always been my passion, but the drive to actually create something, to put the pieces together... it's just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you felt depressed for at least two weeks but did not have the voices, unusual smells, or fears of being watched?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not that I can recall... it really didn’t happen like that. Actually, it was quite the opposite, if I'm being honest. When I think back to those worst months, you know, when that heavy, heavy sadness was just pressing down on me so hard that I couldn't even bring myself to care about my bench or my tools... that was exactly when all those other strange things were happening, too. They were all happening at the exact same time. \n\nI’d be sitting there in the shop, just completely drained, feeling that total loss of interest in everything I usually love, and out of nowhere, I would hear my name being called. Just clear as a bell, echoing right off the glass display cases. And there wouldn't be a soul around. It's such an unsettling feeling, being so low and then having your mind play tricks on you like that.\n\nAnd the worry... oh, the worry was just eating me alive right alongside the sadness. I was absolutely, one hundred percent sure that the customers coming in weren't there to look at the jewelry at all. I’d see someone lingering by the front window, maybe looking at a display of turquoise pendants I’d put out, and I just knew—I was completely convinced—that they were checking up on our payments. It’s a tough business, retail, especially with precious metals because the overhead is so astronomical. You have to buy all your silver casting grain and your stones up front from the suppliers, so money is always on your mind anyway. \n\nBut this was different. I’d be sitting there, too depressed to even pick up a pair of pliers, hearing my name in an empty room, and absolutely terrified that every person who walked through the door was secretly auditing my finances or making sure I was paying the bills. It was exhausting. So, no, the depression didn't happen by itself without those other things. It was all just... tangled up together into one big, awful mess during those worst months. I couldn't separate the sadness from the voices or the fear if I tried.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the woman's voice call your name, does it ever say anything about you being worthless or guilty?\n\nPatient: Worthless or guilty? Oh, no... no, nothing like that at all. It’s actually quite brief, really. It’s literally just my name. \n\nI’ll be sitting there at the bench—usually, it happens when the shop is very quiet, you know, like on a Tuesday afternoon when foot traffic is just completely dead and I'm trying to get caught up on my finishing work. I use this Foredom flex shaft tool with these little silicone polishing wheels, and even though the motor has a bit of a hum to it, I'll just hear the voice cut right through it. Just my name, clear as day. \n\nBut it's never cruel or mean. It doesn't tell me I'm a bad person or that I'm failing at the business or anything awful like that. Honestly, the guilt I feel myself about not being productive enough right now, about just staring at my trays and not making anything... well, that's already heavy enough on its own. I certainly do a fine enough job making myself feel guilty, I don't need a voice doing it for me! \n\nBut the voice itself, no. It’s just a woman, sounding almost exactly like a customer who just wandered in from the street and is trying to get my attention from the front display cases while I'm working in the back. Like she's just stepping out of the rain and calling out to see if anyone is minding the store. It’s never full sentences or anything critical or degrading like you’re asking about. It's just... just my name, hanging there in the air, and then when I get up and walk out to the showroom to see who it is, wiping the polishing compound off my hands, there's absolutely no one there. But no, it never says I'm worthless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts or putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? Or putting them in my head? Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that. I mean, I know my thoughts are my own, even when they're... well, even when they're a bit jumbled or when that heavy sadness takes over. \n\nI know some people talk about things like that, feeling like they aren't in control of their own minds, but for me, it's really just... it's just me in here. Sometimes I wish someone else would take over, honestly, especially when I'm sitting at my bench trying to figure out a new setting for a cabochon and I just can't focus. I'll be staring at a piece of silver bezel wire, knowing exactly how I need to measure it and cut it and solder it, but my brain just feels like it's wading through molasses. And then I start thinking about the suppliers and the bills and whether those people looking in the window are checking up on me... but those are definitely my thoughts. My worries. \n\nNo one is forcing them on me or planting them there like seeds. It's just my own mind, running away with itself, or... or just grinding to a complete halt, depending on the day. But it's all me. Just me and the quiet in the shop, and occasionally that voice calling my name, but even then, my thoughts are my own. I've never felt like I was a puppet or anything like that. Just... very tired, and very overwhelmed by my own mind, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed mood lasted for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, my... it's been much, much longer than that. It feels like it's been stretching on forever, honestly. It's definitely been going on for weeks and weeks in a row without a break. \n\nEvery single day, it's just the same thing. Most mornings, I wake up and there's just this... this flat, heavy sadness sitting right on my chest. It's like the color has completely drained out of the house before I even get out of bed, let alone before I even make it down to the shop. You know how when you wake up and the sun is shining and you think, 'Oh, I'm going to make a pot of coffee and maybe try that new bezel setting technique I saw in that trade magazine'? Well, it's the exact opposite of that. \n\nI'll just lie there, staring at the ceiling fan—it has these little brass accents on the blades that I used to think were so charming when we first moved in, but now they just look dull—and I just feel so incredibly empty and sad. It's a physical weight, I swear it is. And it doesn't lift. It just stays with me while I'm brushing my teeth, while I'm trying to force down a piece of toast, and it follows me all the way to the store. \n\nI unlock the front door, turn off the alarm, flip on the overhead lights over the display cases, and I just stand there feeling completely hollowed out. It doesn't matter if it's a Monday or a Saturday, the feeling is just constant. So, yes, to answer your question, it has absolutely been at least two weeks. It's just a continuous, unrelenting grayness that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you had the voice, the unusual smells, or the feeling of being watched, but were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Well... yes, actually. I think there was. It's funny you ask it that way, because I was just thinking about how things were before this awful, heavy feeling took over everything. \n\nThere was a stretch—I want to say it was earlier this spring, before the depression really settled in and made it impossible to get out of bed. I was actually doing quite well! I was still running my Thursday evening wire-wrapping classes for beginners. I had a full roster, eight ladies, and we were making these lovely little copper and turquoise pendants. And I was gardening on the weekends, too. I had just put in a whole new row of hydrangeas along the side of the house... the big mophead kind, you know? The ones that turn blue if you get the soil acidity just right. I was out there with my trowel and my gardening gloves, feeling perfectly fine, not sad at all. Energetic, even.\n\nBut during that same time, for several weeks, I was having those other things happen. I'd be in the middle of teaching a class, demonstrating how to use round-nose pliers to make a perfect loop, and I'd hear those noises coming from the polishing room in the back. Not just the voice calling my name, but like... shuffling, or the sound of the flex shaft motor whirring for a second when I knew I had unplugged it. And that feeling of being watched... oh, that was so strong then. I kept worrying that customers—or not even customers, just people out on the sidewalk—were staring through the front window, scrutinizing the business, judging how I was running things, maybe even plotting to break in and steal the silver stock. \n\nI was so preoccupied with it that I even went and bought a new deadbolt for the back door near the alley. But my mood? My mood was completely fine. I wasn't crying, I wasn't feeling that terrible gray emptiness. I was just... busy, and gardening, and teaching, but constantly looking over my shoulder and hearing things in the back room. So, yes, there was definitely a period of a few weeks where I had all of that going on, but none of the sadness. It's so strange how the mind works, isn't it? Because now it's all just jumbled together with this awful depression."
